the isomorphism of the statement comes from the canonical isomorphism $h^*(f_*(\sh{O}_U))\xrightarrow{\sim}f'_*(\sh{O}_{U'})$ \sref{IV.2.3.1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[21.12.3]
\label{IV.21.12.3}
For every quasi-compact and quasi-separated morphism $f:U\to X$ and every $x\in X$, one has a canonical isomorphism near
\[
  U^0\times_X\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})=(U\times_X\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x}))^0.
\]
\end{corollary}

It results also from \sref{IV.21.12.2} that one has, for every open $V$ of $X$, a canonical isomorphism near
\[
\label{IV.21.12.4}
  (f^{-1}(V))^0=(f^0)^{-1}(V).
  \tag{21.12.4}
\]

\begin{env}[21.12.5]
\label{IV.21.12.5}
We shall consider in particular the case where $f:U\to X$ is an \emph{open immersion}, $U$ thus being identified with an open subprescheme induced on an open of $X$.
As the morphism $f^{-1}(U)\to U$ restriction of $f^0$ is an isomorphism, $i_U:U\to U^0$ is therefore an \emph{open immersion} allowing to identify $U$ with an open subprescheme induced on an open of $U^0$.

More generally, for an open $V$ of $X$, the restriction $(f^0)^{-1}(V)\to V$ of $f^0$ is an isomorphism of $(f^0)^{-1}(V)$ on the subprescheme induced on the open $V\cap U$ if and only if the open immersion $U\cap V\to V$ is an \emph{affine} morphism.
It is clear $(\sref[II]{II.1.2.1})$ that the reunion of these opens $V$ is the \emph{largest} among them, $U_1$, which contains $U$; by virtue of what precedes, $U_1$ is also the largest open not meeting the set
\[
  \operatorname{Daf}(U/X)=f^0(U^0)-U
\]
(``affinity defect'' of the open $U$ relative to $X$, which is empty if and only if $U$ is affine over $X$);
in other words, the closed set $Z=X-U_1$ is the \emph{adherence} of the set $\operatorname{Daf}(U/X)$.

One will note that for every \emph{flat} morphism $h:X'\to X$, if one sets $U'=h^{-1}(U)$, one has
\[
\label{IV.21.12.5.1}
  \operatorname{Daf}(U'/X')=h^{-1}(\operatorname{Daf}(U/X))
  \tag{21.12.5.1}
\]
as results immediately from \sref{IV.21.12.2.1} and $(\sref[I]{I.3.4.8})$.
In particular, for every open $V$ of $X$, one has
\[
\label{IV.21.12.5.2}
  \operatorname{Daf}((U\cap V)/V)=\operatorname{Daf}(U/X)\cap V
  \tag{21.12.5.2}
\]
and for every $x\in X$,
\[
\label{IV.21.12.5.3}
  \operatorname{Daf}((U\cap\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x}))/\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x}))=\operatorname{Daf}(U/X)\cap\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x}).
  \tag{21.12.5.3}
\]
\end{env}

We shall now, when $X$ is locally Noetherian, give precisions on the nature of $\operatorname{Daf}(U/X)$, which will show for example that when $U$ is everywhere dense in $X$, $\operatorname{Daf}(U/X)$ is not a rare closed set, whatever the case:

\oldpage[IV-4]{306}

\begin{theorem}[21.12.6]
\label{IV.21.12.6}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $U$ an open non-void subset of $X$, $f:U\to X$ the canonical injection.
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\emph{(\roman*)}]
  \item The adherence $Z=X-U_1$ of $\operatorname{Daf}(U/X)$ is of codimension $\geq 2$ in $X$.
  \item If $T=X-U\supset Z$ is of codimension $\geq 2$, the morphism $f^0:U^0\to X$ is surjective.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i) Let us first show that for every $x\in\operatorname{Daf}(U/X)$ one has necessarily $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})>1$.
Indeed, $x$ cannot evidently be a maximal point of $X$, being contained in $\overline{U}-U$;
it is a matter therefore of seeing that one cannot have $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})=1$.
Now, this relation would imply, by \sref{IV.21.12.5.3}, $x\in\operatorname{Daf}(U^{(x)}/\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x}))$, where $U^{(x)}=U\cap\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})$.
But the only opens of $\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})$ are $\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})$ itself and the parts of the set (finite) of maximal points of $\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})$.
Now, the open set reduced to a maximal point of $\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})$ is affine, hence by definition of preschemes;
one concludes that \emph{all} the open sets in $\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})$ are affine, hence $\operatorname{Daf}(U^{(x)}/\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x}))=\emp$ contrary to the hypothesis.

To prove~(i) it must be shown further, to know that if $x\in X$ is such that $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})=1$, there exists an open neighbourhood $W$ of $x$ in $X$ such that $W$ does not meet $\operatorname{Daf}(U/X)$, i.e.\ such that the canonical injection $f_W:U\cap W\to W$ is affine.
But, with the same notations as above, one has just seen that the injection $f^{(x)}:U^{(x)}\to\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,x})$ is affine.
One can evidently restrict to the case where $X$ is Noetherian;
as the morphism $f$ is of finite presentation, the conclusion results from \sref{IV.8.10.5}[, (viii)] applied following the method of \sref{IV.8.1.2}[, $a$]).

(ii) It is a matter of proving that for every point $x\in T$, one has $x\in f^0(U^0)$;
we shall first show that one can reduce to the case where $X=\Spec(A)$, where $A$ is a complete local Noetherian ring, and $x$ the closed point of $X$.
For this, it suffices to make the base change $h:X'=\Spec(A)\to X$, where $A=\hat{\sh{O}}_{X,x}$;
if one sets $U'=h^{-1}(U)$, $f'=f_{(X')}$ is the canonical injection $U'\to X'$, and since the morphism $h$ is \emph{flat}, it results from \sref{IV.21.12.2} that if one proves that $x$ belongs to $f'^0(U'^0)$, one will deduce that $x\in f^0(U^0)$.
By virtue of \sref{IV.6.1.1}, one has operated the sought reduction.

\oldpage[IV-4]{307}

Let then $X_1$ be a closed irreducible subprescheme of $X$ having for underlying space an irreducible component of $X$, of \emph{maximal} dimension, among those that contain a maximal component of $T$, and set $U_1=U\cap X_1$, $T_1=T\cap X_1=X_1-U_1$;
one has therefore $\operatorname{codim}(T_1,X_1)\geq 2$ $(\mathbf{0}$, 14.2.1), and the couple $(U_1,X_1)$ satisfies the same hypotheses as the couple $(U,X)$ ($X_1$ being the spectrum of a quotient ring of $A$, hence local Noetherian and complete).
One has moreover a commutative diagram
\[
  \xymatrix{
    U_1\ar[r]^{j}\ar[d]_{h_1} & U\ar[d]^{f}\\
    X_1\ar[r]_{i} & X
  }
\]
where $i$ and $j$ are the canonical injections, $i$ being therefore an \emph{affine} morphism;
one deduces from \sref{IV.21.12.1} the existence of a morphism $j^0:U_1^0\to U^0$ making commutative the diagram
\[
  \xymatrix{
    U_1^0\ar[r]^{j^0}\ar[d]_{f_1^0} & U^0\ar[d]^{f^0}\\
    X_1\ar[r]_{i} & X
  }
\]
and hence, to prove that $x\in f^0(U^0)$, it suffices to prove that $x\in f_1^0(U_1^0)$.
One can therefore replace $X$ by $X_1$, and one can consequently suppose that the ring $A$ is moreover \emph{integral}.
But $A$ is a quotient of a regular local Noetherian ring by virtue of the theorem of Cohen $(\mathbf{0}$, 19.8.8, (i)) and since it is integral, one can apply \sref{IV.5.11.1} with the family $(x_\alpha)$ reduced to the single maximal point of $X$;
since $\operatorname{codim}(T,X)\geq 2$ by hypothesis, on can suppose that $U$ contains none of the irreducible components of $Y$ whose adherence contains $y$.
By replacing $X$ by $X'=X-\bigcup_i T_i$, one sees that the image by $g$ of $X'-X'_y$ does not contain the images $g(t_i)$, hence does not contain any of the irreducible components of $Y'$ whose adherence contains $y$.
As $X$ and $Y$ are affine, the morphism $g:X\to Y$ is affine, hence so is $f_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is a \emph{coherent} $\sh{O}_X$-Module, hence the morphism $f^0:U^0\to X$ is \emph{finite};
as this morphism is dominant ($U$ being everywhere dense), it is surjective $(\sref[II]{II.6.1.10})$, and hence $x\in f^0(U^0)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[21.12.7]
\label{IV.21.12.7}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $U$ a sub-prescheme induced on an open of $X$, $j:U\to X$ the canonical immersion; suppose that $j$ is an affine morphism.
Then every irreducible component of $T=X-U$ is of codimension $\leq 1$ \emph{(and in particular is of codimension $1$ if $U$ is everywhere dense)}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Suppose indeed that one of the irreducible components $T_1$ of $T$ is of codimension $\geq 2$ in $X$.
Replacing $X$ if needed by a neighbourhood of the generic point of $T_1$, one can suppose $T$ irreducible and of codimension $\geq 2$.
But then the hypothesis of \sref{IV.21.12.6}[, (ii)] implies that $j^0$ must be surjective.
But then $j:U\to X$ is affine implies that $U^0$ is identified with $U$ and $j^0$ with $j$, in other words $j^0(U^0)=U$;
or, this contradicts the conclusion of \sref{IV.21.12.6} that, under the hypothesis of dimension made, $j^0$ must be surjective.
\end{proof}

\oldpage[IV-4]{308}

\begin{env}[21.12.8]
\label{IV.21.12.8}
Let $A$ be a local Noetherian ring, $Y=\Spec(A)$, $y$ the unique closed point of $Y$, $Y'=Y-\{y\}$.
Consider the following condition:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textbf{(W)}] \emph{For every open $U$ of $Y$ contained in $Y'$, not containing any irreducible component of $Y'$ and such that the canonical immersion $U\to Y'$ is affine, $U$ is itself an affine open.}
\end{enumerate}

This condition is implied by the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textbf{(W$'$)}] \emph{For every closed part $T$ of $Y$ whose every irreducible component is of codimension $1$ in $Y$, and such that for every irreducible component $Y_i$ of $Y$, one has $Y_i\cap T\neq\{y\}$, the open $U=Y-T$ is affine.}
\end{enumerate}

Indeed, if (W$'$) is satisfied and if $U$ is an open of $Y$ satisfying the hypothesis of (W), it results from \sref{IV.21.12.7} that no irreducible component of $T=Y-U$ can be of codimension $\geq 2$;
as moreover $U$ contains none of the irreducible components of the open $Y'-\{y\}$ of $Y'$, the condition (W$'$) shows that $U$ is affine.

One will note that condition (W$'$) when $Y$ is irreducible and is then equivalent to the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textbf{(W$''$)}] \emph{For every irreducible closed part $T$ of $Y$, of codimension $1$ in $Y$, $Y-T$ is an affine open.}
\end{enumerate}

Indeed, it is clear that (W$'$) implies (W$''$) when $Y$ is irreducible, and the reciprocal comes from considering the irreducible components of $T$ and noting that the intersection of a finite number of affine opens is an affine open $(\sref[I]{I.5.5.6})$.
\end{env}

\begin{env}[21.12.9]
\label{IV.21.12.9}
\emph{Examples} (21.12.9). ---
If $A$ is a local Noetherian \emph{factorial} ring, it satisfies (W$'$) (and \emph{a fortiori} (W)), since every prime ideal of height $1$ is principal $(\sref[I]{I.1.3.6})$.
But there exist local Noetherian rings that are not factorial satisfying (W$'$), for example those of dimension $\leq 1$:
indeed, one has observed in the proof of \sref{IV.21.12.6}[, (i)] that \emph{all} the opens of $Y$ are then affine.
One can on the other hand prove using the theory of local duality (chap.~III, 3\textsuperscript{e} part) that every local Noetherian ring of dimension $2$ satisfies (W$'$).
\end{env}

\begin{proposition}[21.12.10]
\label{IV.21.12.10}
Let $X$, $Y$ be two locally Noetherian preschemes, $g:X\to Y$ a morphism, $y$ a closed point of $Y$, $Y'=Y-\{y\}$, $X'=g^{-1}(Y')$;
suppose that the morphism $g':X'\to Y'$ restriction of $g$ is an open immersion, and that the local ring $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$ satisfies condition~\emph{(W)} \sref{IV.21.12.8}.
Then, for every irreducible component $Z$ of $X_y=g^{-1}(y)$, either $Z$ is of codimension $\leq 1$ in $X$, or its generic point is isolated in $Z$.
If $Z$ is locally of finite type over $k(y)$, the second alternative implies that $Z$ is reduced to a single point.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The last assertion of the statement results from this that $Z$ is then a prescheme of Jacobson \sref{IV.10.4.7}, and as the set of closed points of $Z$ is then dense in $Z$, the generic point of $Z$ cannot be isolated in $Z$ unless $Z$ is reduced to a single point.

Suppose that there exists in $X_y$ an irreducible component $Z$ whose generic point $z$ is not isolated in $Z$ and such that $\operatorname{codim}(Z,X)\geq 2$.
The question being local on $X$ and $Y$, one can, by replacing $X$ by a neighbourhood of $z$ in $X$, suppose $X$ and $Y$ affine, $X_y=Z$ \emph{irreducible, not reduced to a point} and such that $\operatorname{codim}(Z,X)\geq 2$.
The image $g(X-X_y)=U$ is an open of $Y'$ isomorphic to $X'$ by hypothesis;
let us show by replacing if needed $X$ by a neighbourhood of $y$, that $U$ does not contain any of the irreducible components of $Y'$ whose adherence contains $y$.
Indeed, one can first suppose that all the maximal points of $X$ are generisations of $z$, the set of these points being finite;
hence the set of maximal points of $U$ is the set of images $y_i$ by $g$ of the maximal points $x_i$ of $X$ (no maximal point of $X$ can be contained in $X_y$, since $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 2$).
Setting $X_i=\overline{\{x_i\}}$, since by hypothesis, one has a $z\in X_i$ and since $z$ is not isolated in $X_y$, there exists in $X_y$ a point $x\neq z$.
As $X_i\neq Z$, there exists in $X_i$ a point $t_i$ such that if one sets $T_i=\overline{\{t_i\}}$, one has $\dim(\sh{O}_{T_i,z})=1$ (10.5.9);
this implies by hypothesis $t_i\notin Z$, hence $t_i$ is not a generisation of $z$.
Replacing $X$ by $X'=X-\bigcup_i T_i$, one sees that the image by $g$ of $X'-X'_y$ does not contain the images $g(t_i)$, hence does not contain any of the irreducible components of $Y'$ whose adherence contains $y$.
Since $X$ and $Y$ are affine, the morphism $g:X\to Y$ is affine, hence so is the restriction $g':X'\to Y'$ which is by hypothesis an immersion, hence the canonical immersion $U\to Y'$ is affine.
In view of condition (W), $U=U\cap Y'$ is then an affine open.
Setting $Y_1=\Spec(\sh{O}_{Y,y})$, $Y'_1=Y_1-\{y\}$, $U_1=U\cap Y_1$, one has therefore $\operatorname{codim}(T_1,X_1)\geq 2$, and the couple $(U_1,X_1)$ satisfies the same hypotheses.
We have a commutative diagram, and by \sref{IV.21.12.1} the existence of a morphism $j^0:U_1^0\to U^0$.
But $A$ is a quotient of a regular local ring by the theorem of Cohen, and since it is integral, one can apply \sref{IV.5.11.1};
since $\operatorname{codim}(T,X)\geq 2$, $f_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is a coherent $\sh{O}_X$-Module, hence the morphism $f^0:U^0\to X$ is finite;
as this morphism is dominant, it is surjective $(\sref[II]{II.6.1.10})$, and hence $x\in f^0(U^0)$; Q.E.D.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[21.12.11]
\label{IV.21.12.11}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $U$ a sub-prescheme induced on an open of $X$, $j:U\to X$ the canonical immersion;
suppose that $j$ is an affine morphism.
Then every irreducible component of $T=X-U$ is of codimension $\leq 1$ \emph{(and in particular is of codimension $1$ if $U$ is everywhere dense)}.
\end{corollary}

\oldpage[IV-4]{310}

\begin{theorem}[21.12.12]
\label{IV.21.12.12}
\textnormal{(van der Waerden)}
Let $X$, $Y$ be two locally Noetherian integral preschemes, $g:X\to Y$ a birational morphism and locally of finite type.
Suppose moreover that $Y$ is \emph{normal} and that for every $y\in Y$, the local ring $\sh{O}_{Y,y}$ satisfies condition~\emph{(W)} of \sref{IV.21.12.8} (conditions satisfied in particular when $Y$ is \emph{locally factorial} \sref{IV.21.12.9}).
If $V$ is the largest open of $X$ such that the restriction $g|V:V\to Y$ is a local isomorphism, then all the irreducible components of $T=X-V$ are of codimension $1$ in $X$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
One will note that since $g$ is birational, the open $V$ is not empty;
it therefore suffices to prove that no maximal point $z$ of $T$ can be isolated in $X_y$, where $y=g(z)$.
But, by restricting to a neighbourhood of $z$, one can suppose $T=X_y$;
as moreover $z\in X_y$, one can restrict to the case where $T=X_y$, and one can suppose $g:X\to Y$ is an affine morphism.
On the other hand, since $A$ is integral, $A$ is a discrete valuation ring, hence regular, and the hypothesis $\mathfrak{m}_{x'}\notin\operatorname{Ass}(\sh{O}_{X',x'})$ implies $A'$ is Cohen--Macaulay $(\mathbf{0}$, 16.4.6);
as $\dim(A')=1$, it results from \sref{IV.6.1.5} that $A'$ is flat.
Then all the fibres $g^{-1}(y')$ $(y'\in Y)$ would be empty or reduced to a point and it would result from the ``Main theorem'' (8.12.10) that $g$ would be a local isomorphism, contrary to the hypothesis $z\in T$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[21.12.13]
\label{IV.21.12.13}
Suppose satisfied the hypotheses of \sref{IV.21.12.12} and moreover, suppose that $g$ is quasi-finite in each of the points of $X^{(1)}$ (one recalls that these are the points $x$ where $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})=1$);
then $g$ is a local isomorphism, and if moreover $g$ is separated, $g$ is an open immersion.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to prove the first assertion, the second being a consequence of the first and of $(\sref[I]{I.8.2.8})$.
Everything reduces to proving, with the notations of \sref{IV.21.12.12}, that $T=\emp$;
in the contrary case, a generic point $z$ of an irreducible component of $T$ would belong to $X^{(1)}$ by virtue of \sref{IV.21.12.12}, and by hypothesis it would be isolated in $X_y$ with $y=g(z)$;
but one has seen in the proof of \sref{IV.21.12.12} that this is not possible.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[21.12.14]
\label{IV.21.12.14}
(i) The conclusion of \sref{IV.21.12.12} applies when $Y$ is \emph{regular} and integral and $X$ integral, by virtue of the theorem of Auslander--Buchsbaum \sref{IV.21.11.1}, $Y$ being then locally factorial.
On the other hand, one knows examples where $X$ and $Y$ are normal algebraic schemes of dimension $3$, over an algebraically closed field of characteristic $3$, and where the conclusion of \sref{IV.21.12.12} is no longer valid.

(ii) The set $T$ of \sref{IV.21.12.12} is also the set of points where $g$ is \emph{ramified}.
Indeed, if $g$ is unramified at a point $x\in X$, it is also unramified in a neighbourhood affine open $U$ of $x$ \sref{IV.17.3.7}, hence $g|U$ is a separated, quasi-finite and birational morphism \sref{IV.17.4.3};
since $Y$ is normal, one concludes from the ``Main theorem'' $(\sref[III]{III.4.4.9})$ that $g$ is a local isomorphism at the point $x$, hence $x\in X-T$.
Reciprocally, $g$ is evidently unramified at every point where it is a local isomorphism.
This justifies the title given to this section.

(iii) Without supposing that $Y$ is locally factorial, but supposing on the other hand that $X$ is a complete intersection relative to $Y$ (chap.~V), we will see in chap.~V that one has a more precise result than \sref{IV.21.12.12}, expressing $T$ as a 1-codimensional cycle of a section of an invertible $\sh{O}_X$-Module, canonically associated to $g$.
This applies notably when $X$ and $Y$ are both regular, or when $g$ is an $S$-morphism, $X$ and $Y$ being smooth preschemes over $S$.

(iv) One can ask whether \sref{IV.21.12.10} admits a converse; in other words, for a given local Noetherian ring $A$, if one sets $Y=\Spec(A)$, $y$ denoting the closed point of $Y$, and if, for every locally Noetherian prescheme $X$ and every morphism $g:X\to Y$ such that $g':X'\to Y'$ is an open immersion, every irreducible component of $X_y$ is of codimension $\leq 1$ in $X$ or is reduced to a point, then is it true that $A$ satisfies condition~(W) of \sref{IV.21.12.8}?

(v) Let $Y$ be a regular integral prescheme, $X$ a locally Noetherian normal prescheme, $g:X\to Y$ a morphism locally of finite type; suppose moreover that, if $\eta$ is the generic point of $Y$, the fibre $X_\eta$ is étale over $k(\eta)$, and let $T'$ be the complement of the largest open $V'$ of $X$ such that $g|V':V'\to Y$ is étale; is it true then that all the irreducible components of $T'$ are of codimension $1$?
This is indeed the case when one supposes moreover that $g$ is locally quasi-finite (``purity theorem'' of Zariski--Nagata).
One can show that the preceding conjecture would be a consequence of the following (and would even be equivalent to it if the conjecture of~(iv) were verified): for a regular local ring $A$ contained in an integral and integrally closed local ring $B$, such that $B$ is a finite $A$-module, the open $U$ of the points of $\Spec(B)$ at which $\Spec(B)$ is unramified over $\Spec(A)$ (or, what amounts to the same by \sref{IV.18.10.1}, étale over $\Spec(A)$) is affine.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[21.12.15]
\label{IV.21.12.15}
Let $S$ be a prescheme, $g:X\to S$ a flat morphism and locally of finite presentation, whose fibres $X_s=g^{-1}(s)$ are geometrically irreducible \sref{IV.4.5.2}, $h:Y\to S$ a smooth morphism; we denote by $Y_s=h^{-1}(s)$ the fibres of this morphism.
Let $f:X\to Y$ be a proper $S$-morphism such that, for every maximal point $\eta$ of $S$, the morphism $f_\eta:X_\eta\to Y_\eta$ is an isomorphism.
Then $f$ is an isomorphism.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $h$ is flat, every maximal point of $Y$ is above a maximal point of $S$ \sref{IV.2.3.4}, hence the union of the $Y_\eta$, as $\eta$ runs through the maximal points of $S$, is dense in $Y$; as the $f_\eta$ are isomorphisms, $f$ is dominant and hence surjective since it is proper.
By \sref{IV.17.9.5}, it suffices to prove that for every $s\in S$, $f_s:X_s\to Y_s$ is an open immersion.
As $s$ is a generisation of a maximal point $\eta_s$, one can, by making the base change $\Spec(\sh{O}_{S,s})\to S$, reduce to the case where $S$ is the spectrum of a local integral Noetherian ring $A_0$ of $A=\sh{O}_{S,s}$ such that $A_0\to A$ is a local homomorphism, two morphisms of finite type $g_0:X_0\to S_0=\Spec(A_0)$, $h_0:Y_0\to S_0$ and an $S_0$-morphism $f_0:X_0\to Y_0$ such that $g$, $h$, $f$ are deduced from $g_0$, $h_0$, $f_0$ by the base change $S\to S_0$ $((8.9.1)$ and $(5.13.3))$; one can moreover suppose $g_0$ flat \sref{IV.11.2.7}, $h_0$ smooth \sref{IV.17.7.9} and $f_0$ proper $(8.10.5,\text{ (xii)})$.
On the other hand, the projection $S_0$ of the generic point $\eta$ of $S$ is the closed point of $S_0$, and it results from \sref{IV.2.7.1}, (viii)) that the morphism $(f_0)_{\eta_0}:(X_0)_{\eta_0}\to(Y_0)_{\eta_0}$ is an isomorphism; finally, the closed point $s_0$ of $S_0$ is above the generic point $\eta$ of $S$, hence one can reduce to the case where moreover $S$ is Noetherian.

On the other hand, the projection of the generic point $\eta$ of $S$ is the closed point of $S_0$;
we can therefore reduce to the case where $S$ is the spectrum of a discrete valuation ring.
One can then finish by reducing to the case where $Y$ is integral.
Since $g:X\to S$ is flat, the points $x$ of $X$ are contained in $X_\eta$ \sref{IV.2.3.4} and since $f_\eta$ is an isomorphism, $X$ is irreducible and the local ring at its generic point is a field; moreover, by flatness $(3.3.2)$, $X$ has no embedded cycles, hence $X$ is reduced \sref{IV.3.2.1} and hence $f$ is a birational morphism \emph{and separated}.
To prove that $f$ is an open immersion, one can apply the criterion \sref{IV.21.12.13}; to see that $f$ is quasi-finite at the points of $X^{(1)}$, one remarks that the assertion is evident for those of these points that belong to $X_\eta$; the only point of $X^{(1)}$ belonging to $X_s$ is the generic point $x$ of $X_s$ \sref{IV.6.1.1}.
Now, it results from \sref{IV.2.4.6} and of \sref{IV.14.2.2} that the morphisms $g$ and $h$ are equidimensional; as $X_\eta$ and $Y_\eta$ are isomorphic, the irreducible components of $X_s$ and of $Y_s$ have all the same dimension.
But since by hypothesis $X_s$ is irreducible and as one has seen that $f$ is surjective, hence it is also the case for $f_s:X_s\to Y_s$, which entails that $Y_s$ is also irreducible.
Hence $f(x)=y$, and the relation $\dim(X_s)=\dim(Y_s)$ entails then, by virtue of \sref{IV.5.6.6}, that $\dim(f_s^{-1}(y))=0$, which says that $f_s$ (and hence also $f$) is quasi-finite at the point $x$, which finishes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[21.12.16]
\label{IV.21.12.16}
Let $g:X\to S$ be a proper morphism, flat and of finite presentation, $h:Y\to S$ a smooth morphism, proper and of finite presentation, $f:X\to Y$ an $S$-morphism.
Suppose that the fibres $X_s=g^{-1}(s)$ of $g$ are geometrically irreducible.
Let $U$ be the set of $s\in S$ such that $f_s:X_s\to Y_s$ is an isomorphism.
Then $U$ is open and closed in $S$, and the morphism $g^{-1}(U)\to h^{-1}(U)$, restriction of $f$, is an isomorphism.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The last assertion results from the first and from \sref{IV.17.9.5}.
We already know \sref{IV.9.6.1}, (xi)) that $U$ is locally constructible in $S$.
By virtue of \sref{IV.1.10.1}, it suffices to prove that $U$ is stable by \emph{specialisation} and by \emph{generisation}.
To prove these assertions, one reasons as in the proof of \sref{IV.21.12.15} (noting, with the same notations, that the closed point of $S$ is the projection of the closed point of $S_0$) to reduce to the case where moreover $S$ is Noetherian; then the conclusion results from \sref[III]{III.4.6.7}, (ii)).
\end{proof}

\oldpage[IV-4]{313}

\begin{remark}[21.12.17]
\label{IV.21.12.17}
(i) The conclusion of the proposition \sref{IV.21.12.15} is no longer valid if one eliminates the hypothesis that the fibres $X_s$ are irreducible.
Take for example $S=\Spec(A)$, where $A$ is a discrete valuation ring, $Y=\bb{P}_k^1$, which is proper and smooth over $S$ \sref{IV.17.3.9}.
Let $s$ and $\eta$ be the closed point and the generic point of $S$; let $z$ be a closed point of $Y_s$, say a rational point over $k=k(s)$, and let $X$ be the $Y$-prescheme obtained by blowing up $z$ in the construction of \sref{IV.15.1.1.6} that, if $f:X\to Y$ is the structural morphism, $f^{-1}(z)$ is isomorphic to $\bb{P}_k^1$, and on the other hand, the complement of $f^{-1}(z)$ in $X_s$, i.e.\ the two irreducible components of $X_s$.
Since $X$ is integral $(\sref[II]{II.8.1.4})$ and for every affine open $U$ of $X$, the homomorphism $A\to\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_X)$ is injective \sref{IV.1.2.7}, hence $\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_X)$ is an $A$-module without torsion, and hence flat since $A$ is a discrete valuation ring $(\mathbf{0}_1, 6.3.4)$.
It is clear that $f_\eta:X_\eta\to Y_\eta$ is an isomorphism, though $f_s:X_s\to Y_s$ is not.

(ii) The conclusion of \sref{IV.21.12.15} also fails if, under the hypotheses, one drops the hypothesis that $f$ is \emph{proper}.
It suffices to take to see this to define $S$ and $Y$ as in~(i), $f$ by the restriction $f':X'\to Y$ of $f$; the morphism $g':X'\to S$, restriction of $g$, is still flat, and this time $X'_s$ is geometrically irreducible; $X'_s$ is moreover isomorphic to the complement of a closed point in $\bb{V}_k^1$; hence $X'_s$ is an affine scheme isomorphic to $\bb{A}_k^1$; as its image in $Y_s$ is reduced to the point $z$, $f'$ is not an isomorphism, even though $f'$ is one.

(iii) It is possible that the statement of the proposition \sref{IV.21.12.15} remains valid when one replaces the word ``isomorphism'' by ``étale morphism'' (cf.\ \sref{IV.21.12.14},~(v)).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Parafactorial pairs. Parafactorial local rings.}
\label{subsection:IV.21.13}

\begin{definition}[21.13.1]
\label{IV.21.13.1}
Let $X$ be a ringed space, $Y$ a closed subset of $X$, $U=X-Y$.
We say that the couple $(X,Y)$ is \emph{parafactorial} if, for every open $V$ of $X$, the restriction functor $\sh{L}\mapsto\sh{L}|(U\cap V)$, from the category of invertible $\sh{O}_V$-Modules to that of invertible $\sh{O}_{U\cap V}$-Modules, is an equivalence of categories.
\end{definition}

To say that the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial thus means that, for every open $V$ of $X$, the following conditions are satisfied:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[$1\textsuperscript{o}$] the functor $\sh{L}\mapsto\sh{L}|(U\cap V)$ is \emph{fully faithful}, in other words, for two invertible $\sh{O}_V$-Modules $\sh{L}$, $\sh{L}'$, the restriction map
\[
  \Hom_{\sh{O}_V}(\sh{L},\sh{L}')\to\Hom_{\sh{O}_{U\cap V}}(\sh{L}|(U\cap V),\sh{L}'|(U\cap V))
\]
is bijective;
  \item[$2\textsuperscript{o}$] the functor $\sh{L}\mapsto\sh{L}|(U\cap V)$ is \emph{essentially surjective}, in other words, for every invertible $\sh{O}_{U\cap V}$-Module $\sh{L}_0$, there exists an invertible $\sh{O}_V$-Module $\sh{L}$ such that $\sh{L}_0$ is isomorphic to $\sh{L}|(U\cap V)$; this is also expressed by saying that the canonical homomorphism \sref{IV.21.3.2.4}
\[
  \operatorname{Pic}(V)\to\operatorname{Pic}(U\cap V)
\]
is \emph{surjective}.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{lemma}[21.13.2]
\label{IV.21.13.2}
Let $f:X'\to X$ be a morphism of ringed spaces; for every open $V$ of $X$, we denote by $f_V:f^{-1}(V)\to V$ the restriction of $f$.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[a)] For every open $V$ of $X$, the functor $\sh{E}\mapsto f_V^*(\sh{E})$ from the category of $\sh{O}_V$-Modules locally free of finite rank to the category of $\sh{O}_{f^{-1}(V)}$-Modules locally free of finite rank, is faithful \emph{(resp.\ fully faithful)}.
  \item[a${}'$)] For every open $V$ of $X$, the functor $\sh{L}\mapsto f_V^*(\sh{L})$ from the category of $\sh{O}_V$-Modules locally free of rank $1$ to the category of $\sh{O}_{f^{-1}(V)}$-Modules locally free of rank $1$ is faithful \emph{(resp.\ fully faithful)}.
  \item[b)] For every open $V$ of $X$, the canonical homomorphism $(\mathbf{0}_I, 4.4.3.2)$ $\sh{E}\to(f_V)_*(f_V^*(\sh{E}))$ is a monomorphism \emph{(resp.\ an isomorphism)} for every $\sh{O}_V$-Module locally free of finite rank $\sh{E}$.
  \item[b${}'$)] The canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to f_*(\sh{O}_{X'})$ is a monomorphism \emph{(resp.\ an isomorphism)}.
\end{enumerate}

Suppose that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to f_*(\sh{O}_{X'})$ is bijective; then, for an $\sh{O}_{X'}$-Module locally free of finite rank $\sh{E}'$ to be isomorphic to an $\sh{O}_{X'}$-Module of the form $f^*(\sh{E})$, where $\sh{E}$ is an $\sh{O}_X$-Module locally free of finite rank, it is necessary and sufficient that the two following conditions be satisfied:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] $f_*(\sh{E}')$ is an $\sh{O}_X$-Module locally free;
  \item[(ii)] the canonical homomorphism $(\mathbf{0}_I, 4.4.3.3)$ $f^*(f_*(\sh{E}'))\to\sh{E}'$ is an isomorphism.
\end{enumerate}
When these two conditions are satisfied, $\sh{E}$ is isomorphic to $f_*(\sh{E}')$.
\end{lemma}

\oldpage[IV-4]{314}

To say that the functor $\sh{E}\mapsto f_V^*(\sh{E})$ is faithful (resp.\ fully faithful) means that for two $\sh{O}_V$-Modules locally free of finite rank $\sh{E}_1$, $\sh{E}_2$, the map
$\Hom_{\sh{O}_V}(\sh{E}_1,\sh{E}_2)\to\Hom_{\sh{O}_{f^{-1}(V)}}(f^*(\sh{E}_1),f^*(\sh{E}_2))$ is injective (resp.\ bijective); as this must hold on replacing $V$ by an open $W\subset V$ and $\sh{E}_1$, $\sh{E}_2$ by $\sh{E}_1|W$, $\sh{E}_2|W$, and as $\Hom_{\sh{O}_W}(\sh{E}_1|W,\sh{E}_2|W)=\Gamma(W,\sh{H}om_{\sh{O}_V}(\sh{E}_1,\sh{E}_2))$, the condition means also that the canonical sheaf homomorphism
\begin{equation*}
\tag{21.13.2.1}
  \sh{H}om_{\sh{O}_V}(\sh{E}_1,\sh{E}_2)\to(f_V)_*(f_V^*(\sh{H}om_{\sh{O}_V}(\sh{E}_1,\sh{E}_2)))
\end{equation*}
is injective (resp.\ bijective).
But since $\sh{E}_1$ and $\sh{E}_2$ are locally free of finite rank, $\sh{H}om_{\sh{O}_V}(\sh{E}_1,\sh{E}_2)$, isomorphic to $\sh{E}_1^\vee\otimes_{\sh{O}_V}\sh{E}_2$ $(\mathbf{0}_I, 5.4.2)$ is also locally of finite rank; this already shows that $a)$ implies $b)$, and conversely $b')$ is a particular case of $a)$, taking into account the isomorphism $\sh{E}^\vee\cong\sh{H}om_{\sh{O}_V}(\sh{E},\sh{O}_V)$.
It is clear that $a')$ is a particular case of $a)$ and $b')$ a particular case of $a')$.
Conversely, as $b)$, the question being local, one can, to verify it, reduce to the case where $\sh{E}=\sh{O}_V^n$, and this shows that $b')$.

If the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to f_*(\sh{O}_{X'})$ is bijective, and if conditions~(i) and~(ii) are satisfied, it is clear that $\sh{E}'=f^*(\sh{E})$ with $\sh{E}=f_*(\sh{E}')$, up to isomorphism.
Conversely, suppose that $\sh{E}'=f^*(\sh{E})$, with $\sh{E}$ locally free; the question being local on $X$, one can suppose that $\sh{E}=\sh{O}_X^n$, whence $\sh{E}'=\sh{O}_{X'}^n$, and conditions~(i) and~(ii) result from the hypothesis that $\sh{O}_X\to f_*(\sh{O}_{X'})$ is an isomorphism.

In this number, we will apply the preceding lemma to a canonical injection $j:U\to X$, where $U$ is the ringed space induced by $X$ on an \emph{open} of $X$.
With these notations, \sref{IV.21.13.2} translates as:

\begin{corollary}[21.13.3]
\label{IV.21.13.3}
Let $X$ be a ringed space, $Y$ a closed subset of $X$, $U=X-Y$, $j:U\to X$ the canonical injection.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[a)] For every open $V$ of $X$, the restriction functor $\sh{E}\mapsto\sh{E}|(U\cap V)$ from the category of $\sh{O}_V$-Modules locally free of finite rank to the category of $\sh{O}_{U\cap V}$-Modules locally free of finite rank is faithful \emph{(resp.\ fully faithful)}.
  \item[a${}'$)] For every open $V$ of $X$, the restriction functor $\sh{L}\mapsto\sh{L}|(U\cap V)$ from the category of $\sh{O}_V$-Modules locally free of rank $1$ to the category of $\sh{O}_{U\cap V}$-Modules locally free of rank $1$ is faithful \emph{(resp.\ fully faithful)}.
  \item[b)] For every open $V$ of $X$, the canonical homomorphism $\sh{E}\to(j_V)_*(\sh{E}|(U\cap V))$ is injective \emph{(resp.\ bijective)}.
  \item[b${}'$)] The canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is injective \emph{(resp.\ bijective)}.
\end{enumerate}

Suppose that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is bijective.
Then, for an $\sh{O}_U$-Module locally free of finite rank $\sh{F}$ to be of the form $\sh{E}|U$, where $\sh{E}$ is an $\sh{O}_X$-Module locally free of finite rank, it is necessary and sufficient that $j_*(\sh{F})$ be an $\sh{O}_X$-Module locally free, and in this case, one can take $\sh{E}=j_*(\sh{F})$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{lemma}[21.13.4]
\label{IV.21.13.4}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $Y$ a closed subset of $X$, $U=X-Y$, $j:U\to X$ the canonical injection.
For the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ to be injective \emph{(resp.\ bijective)}, it is necessary and sufficient that $\operatorname{prof}_Y(\sh{O}_X)\geq 1$ \emph{(resp.\ $\operatorname{prof}_Y(\sh{O}_X)\geq 2$)} (in other words, that $\operatorname{prof}(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 1$ \emph{(resp.\ $\operatorname{prof}(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 2$)} for every $x\in Y$).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
These assertions are indeed particular cases of \sref{IV.5.10.2} and \sref{IV.5.10.5}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.5]
\label{IV.21.13.5}
Let $X$ be a ringed space, $Y$ a closed subset, $U=X-Y$, $j:U\to X$ the canonical injection.
For the couple $(X,Y)$ to be parafactorial, it is necessary and sufficient that for every open $V$ of $X$ and every invertible $\sh{O}_{U\cap V}$-Module $\sh{L}$, $(j_V)_*(\sh{L})$ be an invertible $\sh{O}_V$-Module (notation of \sref{IV.21.13.3}).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is an immediate consequence of the definition \sref{IV.21.13.1} and of \sref{IV.21.13.3}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[21.13.6]
\label{IV.21.13.6}
Let $X$ be a ringed space, $Y$ a closed subset of $X$.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] If the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial, so is $(W,Y\cap W)$ for every open $W$ of $X$.
  Conversely, if $(W_\alpha)$ is an open covering of $X$ such that each of the couples $(W_\alpha,Y\cap W_\alpha)$ is parafactorial, then the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial.
  \item[(ii)] If the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial, so is $(X,Y')$ for every closed subset $Y'$ of $Y$.
  \item[(iii)] Suppose that $X$ is a prescheme, and let $X'$ be a prescheme, $f:X'\to X$ a faithfully flat and quasi-compact morphism; set $Y'=f^{-1}(Y)$.
  Suppose that the couple $(X',Y')$ is parafactorial and that the open $U=X-Y$ is retrocompact in $X$ $(\mathbf{0}_{III}, 9.1.1)$.
  Then the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
(i) As the fact that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is bijective is a local property on $X$, everything reduces to seeing (by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.5}), denoting by $j_\alpha$ the canonical injection $U\cap W_\alpha\to W_\alpha$, that one has the following property: if, for every $\alpha$ and every invertible $\sh{O}_U$-Module $\sh{L}$, $(j_\alpha)_*(\sh{L}|(U\cap W_\alpha))$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_{W_\alpha}$-Module, then $j_*(\sh{L})$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_X$-Module.
But the property of being an invertible $\sh{O}_X$-Module is local on $X$; as one has $j_*(\sh{L})|W_\alpha=(j_\alpha)_*(\sh{L}|(U\cap W_\alpha))$, this proves our assertion.

(ii) Set $U'=X-Y'$ and let $j':U'\to X$ be the canonical injection.
To say that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j'_*(\sh{O}_{U'})$ is bijective amounts to saying that for every open $V$ of $X$, the homomorphism $\Gamma(V,\sh{O}_X)\to\Gamma(V\cap U',\sh{O}_X)$ is bijective; but the composed homomorphism
\[
  \Gamma(V,\sh{O}_X)\to\Gamma(V\cap U',\sh{O}_X)\to\Gamma(V\cap U,\sh{O}_X)
\]
is bijective by hypothesis \sref{IV.21.13.5} and on replacing $V$ by $V\cap U'$, one sees that $\Gamma(V\cap U',\sh{O}_X)\to\Gamma(V\cap U,\sh{O}_X)$ is bijective, hence $\Gamma(V,\sh{O}_X)\to\Gamma(V\cap U',\sh{O}_X)$ is bijective.
Note next that if $j'':U\to U'$ is the canonical injection and if $\sh{L}'$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_{U'}$-Module, then $j_*(\sh{L}'|U)=j'_*(j''_*(\sh{L}'|U))$ is by hypothesis an invertible $\sh{O}_X$-Module.
As the couple $(U',U'\cap Y)$ is parafactorial by virtue of~(i), this proves our assertion.

(iii) Set $U'=X'-Y'$ and let $j':U'\to X'$ be the canonical injection.
The hypothesis that $\sh{O}_{X'}\to j'_*(\sh{O}_{U'})$ is bijective means that for every open $V$ of $X$, the homomorphism $\Gamma(V,\sh{O}_X)\to\Gamma(V\cap U,\sh{O}_X)$ is bijective.
By making the base change $\Spec(\sh{O}_{S,s})\to S$, one can reduce to the case where $S$ is a local scheme, and the conclusion follows from \sref{IV.21.13.5} and from \sref{IV.2.5.2}.
\end{proof}

\oldpage[IV-4]{316}

\begin{definition}[21.13.7]
\label{IV.21.13.7}
We say that a local ring $A$ is \emph{parafactorial} if, setting $X=\Spec(A)$ and denoting by $a$ the closed point of $X$, the couple $(X,\{a\})$ is parafactorial.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.8]
\label{IV.21.13.8}
The notations being those of \sref{IV.21.13.7}, set $U=X-\{a\}$.
For $A$ to be parafactorial, it is necessary and sufficient that it satisfy the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] The canonical homomorphism $A=\Gamma(X,\sh{O}_X)\to\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_X)$ is bijective.
  \item[(ii)] $\operatorname{Pic}(U)=0$.
\end{enumerate}

If moreover $A$ is Noetherian, condition~\emph{(i)} is equivalent to
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i bis)] $\operatorname{prof}(A)\geq 2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, the only open of $X$ containing $a$ is $X$ itself, and hence every invertible $\sh{O}_X$-Module is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_X$, in other words $\operatorname{Pic}(X)=0$; the first assertion results therefore from the definition \sref{IV.21.13.1} and from \sref{IV.21.13.3}.
The second assertion is nothing other than a particular case of \sref{IV.21.13.4}.
\end{proof}

\oldpage[IV-4]{317}

\begin{example}[21.13.9]
\label{IV.21.13.9}
(i) A Noetherian parafactorial local ring is necessarily of dimension $\geq 2$ by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.8}; in other words a Noetherian local ring of dimension $\leq 1$ is not parafactorial.

(ii) A Noetherian \emph{factorial} local ring $A$ of dimension $\geq 2$ is parafactorial, as results from \sref{IV.21.13.8} and from \sref{IV.21.6.14}.

(iii) If $A$ is a Noetherian local ring of dimension $\geq 3$ and parafactorial, it is not necessarily normal nor even reduced.
Consider a regular local ring $B$ of dimension $\geq 3$, and set $A=\operatorname{D}_B(B)$ $(\mathbf{0}, 18.2.3)$, isomorphic to $B[T]/(T^2)$; one sees immediately that one has $\operatorname{prof}(A)=\operatorname{prof}(B)=\dim(B)\geq 3$.
To show that $A$ is parafactorial, it suffices, with the notations of \sref{IV.21.13.8}, to prove that $\operatorname{Pic}(U)=0$.
Let $\mathfrak{J}$ be the kernel of the augmentation $A\to B$, which is such that $\mathfrak{J}^2=0$ and which, as a $B$-module, is isomorphic to $B$.
As $B=A/\mathfrak{J}$, $X=\Spec(A)$ and $X_0=\Spec(B)$ have the same underlying space; if $\sh{J}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}$, $X_0$ is the sub-prescheme defined by $\sh{J}$; we denote by $U_0$ the sub-prescheme induced by $X_0$ on the open $U$.
For every $z\in\mathfrak{J}$, set $\varphi(z)=1+z$; since $(1+z)(1-z)=1$, $1+z$ is invertible in $A$ and $\varphi(\mathfrak{J})$ is the kernel of the surjective canonical homomorphism of multiplicative groups $A^*\to B^*$; in other terms, one has an exact sequence of groups
\[
  0\to\mathfrak{J}\xrightarrow{\varphi}A^*\to B^*\to 1
\]
(the last three groups being multiplicative, the first two additive).
For the same reason, for every $t\in A$, one has an exact sequence
\[
  0\to\mathfrak{J}_t\xrightarrow{\varphi_t}(A_t)^*\to(B_t)^*\to 1
\]
denoting by $t_0$ the image of $t$ in $B$, since $B_t=A_t/\mathfrak{J}_t$; in other words, one has an exact sequence of sheaves of groups on the topological space $X$
\[
  0\to\sh{J}\xrightarrow{u}\sh{O}_X^*\to\sh{O}_{X_0}^*\to 1.
\]
By the exact cohomology sequence, one deduces an exact sequence
\begin{equation*}
\tag{21.13.9.1}
  \mathrm{H}^1(U,\sh{J})\to\mathrm{H}^1(U,\sh{O}_U^*)\xrightarrow{u_*}\mathrm{H}^1(U,\sh{O}_{U_0}^*).
\end{equation*}
But since $\mathfrak{J}$ is a $B$-module isomorphic to $B$, one has $\mathrm{H}^1(U,\sh{J})=\mathrm{H}^1(U_0,\sh{O}_{U_0})$, and it results from chap.~III, 3\textsuperscript{e} partie (see also \cite[III, Exemple III-1]{41}) that one has
\oldpage[IV-4]{318}
$\mathrm{H}^1(U_0,\sh{O}_{U_0})=0$ by reason of the relation $\operatorname{prof}(B)\geq 3$.
Furthermore, as $B$ is factorial, one has $\mathrm{H}^1(U,\sh{O}_{U_0}^*)=\operatorname{Pic}(U_0)=0$; one therefore deduces from the preceding exact sequence that $\operatorname{Pic}(U)=\mathrm{H}^1(U,\sh{O}_U^*)=0$.

(iv) There also exist Noetherian local rings of dimension $3$ that are integral, integrally closed and parafactorial, but not factorial.
Let indeed $B$ be a Noetherian local ring, complete, integral and integrally closed, of dimension $\geq 2$ and not factorial (for example the completion of the localised ring of the integral algebra $k[U,V,W]/(W^2-UV)$ at the image of the ideal $(U)+(V)+(W))$.
Then it results from what one will see further below $(21.14.2)$ that the local ring $A=B[[T]]$ of formal power series in $T$ over $B$ is parafactorial, but it is not factorial, for otherwise $B$ would be so by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.12} below.

(v) One can show that a local ring of absolute complete intersection $(19.3.1)$ of dimension $\geq 4$ is parafactorial (cf.\ \cite[XI, 3.13~(i)]{41}).

(vi) We have seen (Remark~(ii)) that every factorial Noetherian local ring $A$ of dimension $2$ is parafactorial.
But there exist Noetherian local rings of dimension $2$ that are parafactorial and not factorial.
One can show that, for a Noetherian local ring $A$ of dimension $2$ to be parafactorial and not factorial, it suffices that it satisfy the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[$1\textsuperscript{o}$] $A$ is a Cohen--Macaulay ring (in other words $\operatorname{prof}(A)=2$).
  \item[$2\textsuperscript{o}$] $A$ is integral and if $A'$ is its integral closure, $A'$ is factorial and is a finite $A$-algebra.
  \item[$3\textsuperscript{o}$] Let $\mathfrak{J}$ be the conductor of $A$ in $A'$ (annihilator of the $A$-module $A'/A$, or equivalently the largest ideal of $A'$ contained in $A$); set $B=A/\mathfrak{J}$, $B'=A'/\mathfrak{J}$; then $\dim(B)=1$ (which implies $A'\neq A$, in other words $A$ is not integrally closed), and the canonical map $\operatorname{Div}(B)\to\operatorname{Div}(B')$ \sref{IV.21.4.5} is \emph{surjective}.
\end{enumerate}
One can show moreover that these conditions entail the following property:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[$4\textsuperscript{o}$] The ring $B'$ (and \emph{a fortiori} $B\subset B'$) is reduced, and the morphism $\Spec(B')\to\Spec(B)$ is bijective.
\end{enumerate}

If one sets $X=\Spec(A)$, $X'=\Spec(A')$, $Y=\Spec(B)$, $Y'=\Spec(B')$, so that $Y$ (resp.\ $Y'$) is defined by the ideal $\mathfrak{J}$ of $A$ (resp.\ $A'$), the structural morphism $f:X'\to X$ is an isomorphism of $X'-Y'$ onto $X-Y$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~V, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~5, cor.~5 of prop.~16).
One sees then by virtue of $4\textsuperscript{o}$ that $f$ is a \emph{bijective} morphism; in other words, $X$ is a unibranch prescheme \sref{IV.6.15.1} (and in particular $A'$ is a \emph{local} Noetherian ring); in general $X$ is not geometrically unibranch.
The space $Y$, of dimension $1$, is constituted by the closed point $x$ of $X$ and the maximal points $y_i$ $(1\leq i\leq r)$ of $Y$, and the unique point $y'_i$ of $Y'$ above $y_i$ is also a maximal point of $Y'$; as $B$ and $B'$ are reduced, one deduces that one has $\mathfrak{m}_{Y',y'}=\mathfrak{m}_{y'}$ for $z=y_i$ and $z'=y'_i$; this relation is also evidently satisfied for $z\neq Y$ and $z'$ the unique point of $f^{-1}(z)$, hence for every point $z\in U=X-\{x\}$ and $z'$ the unique point of $f^{-1}(z)$.
In particular, if the ring $A$ is of characteristic $0$ $(\mathbf{0}, 11.1.1)$ one sees that $U'$ is \emph{unramified} over $U$ (hence étale in general).

We will limit ourselves here to proving that conditions $1\textsuperscript{o}$, $2\textsuperscript{o}$, $3\textsuperscript{o}$ above are \emph{sufficient} for $A$ to be parafactorial.
By virtue of condition $1\textsuperscript{o}$ and of \sref{IV.21.13.8}, it suffices to prove that $\operatorname{Pic}(U)=0$.
Since $A'$ is factorial by virtue of $2\textsuperscript{o}$, one has $\operatorname{Pic}(U')=0$, and one deduces from \sref{IV.21.8.5},~(ii) a surjection exact sequence
\[
  1\to\bigg(\prod_{s\in S}\sh{O}_{U,s}^*/\sh{O}_{U',s}^*/\operatorname{Im}\Gamma(U',\sh{O}_{U'}^*)\bigg)\to\operatorname{Pic}(U)\to\operatorname{Pic}(U')\to 0
\]
and it thus suffices to show that the second term of this sequence is reduced to the neutral element, by taking $S$ the set of $y_i$ $(1\leq i\leq r)$ and noting that $\sh{O}'_X=f_*(\sh{O}_{X'})$ is the $\sh{O}_X$-Algebra $\widetilde{A'}$.
\end{example}

\oldpage[IV-4]{319}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.10]
\label{IV.21.13.10}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $Y$ a closed subset of $X$.
For the couple $(X,Y)$ to be parafactorial, it is necessary and sufficient that, for every $y\in Y$, the local ring $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ be parafactorial.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Set $U=X-Y$ and let $j:U\to X$ be the canonical injection.
By virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.4}, to say that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is injective is equivalent to saying that each of the local rings $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ for $y\in Y$ satisfies condition~(i~bis) of \sref{IV.21.13.8}.

Let us show first that if the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial, each of the local rings $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ $(y\in Y)$ is parafactorial.
Taking into account the remark from the beginning, everything reduces to seeing that, if one sets $T_y=\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,y})$ and $U_y=T_y-\{y\}$, the hypothesis that $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ is parafactorial entails $\operatorname{Pic}(U_y)=0$; but as $T_y$ is a projective limit of the preschemes induced by $X$ on the affine open neighbourhoods $V-(V\cap\overline{\{y\}})$ of $y$ in $X$, which are quasi-compact since $X$ is locally Noetherian, that the prescheme $U_y$ is a projective limit of preschemes $U_V=V-(V\cap\overline{\{y\}})$ which are separated, it results from $(8.5.2,\text{ (ii)})$ and $(8.5.5)$ that there exists a affine open neighbourhood $V$ of $y$ in $X$ and an $\sh{O}_{U_V}$-Module invertible $\sh{L}'_V$ on $U_V$ inducing $\sh{L}'$ on $U_y$; applying this $(8.5.2)$ and $(8.5.2, b)$) that $j_*(\sh{L})|V_1$ is isomorphic to $\sh{L}_1$, hence invertible.
But this contradicts the definition of $V$, and one thus concludes the proof of \sref{IV.21.13.10}.

Conversely, let us prove that if all the $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ $(y\in Y)$ are parafactorial the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial.
One is evidently reduced, taking into account the remark from the beginning, to showing that for every invertible $\sh{O}_U$-Module $\sh{L}$, $j_*(\sh{L})$ is an invertible $\sh{O}_X$-Module \sref{IV.21.13.5}.
The question being local on $X$, one can suppose $X$ Noetherian.
The set of $x\in X$ such that the restriction of $j_*(\sh{L})$ to a neighbourhood of $x$ in $X$ is invertible is evidently an open in $X$; it suffices to show that $V=X$.
Suppose the contrary and set $Z=X-V\neq\emp$.
Let $y$ be a maximal point of $Z$; since $Z\subset Y$ by definition, $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ is by hypothesis a parafactorial ring.
Replacing $X$ as needed by a neighbourhood of $y$ in $X$, one can suppose that the restriction of $j_*(\sh{L})$ to $V-(V\cap\overline{\{y\}})$ is invertible; hence, with the notations introduced above, the $\sh{O}_{U_y}$-Module induced by $j_*(\sh{L})$ on $U_y$ is invertible.
Since $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ is parafactorial, $\sh{L}_0$ is induced by an $\sh{O}_{T_y}$-Module invertible $\sh{L}'$; but as $T_y$ is projective limit of affine open neighbourhoods $W$ of $y$ in $X$, one can again apply $(8.5.2, \text{(ii)})$ and $(8.5.5)$; replacing $W$ by a smaller neighbourhood of $y$, one can suppose that $\sh{L}_V$ is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_V$, hence $\sh{L}_0$ is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_{U_y}$.
On concludes then, from \sref{IV.21.13.2}, $b')$, that $j_*(\sh{L})$ induces on $W$ an invertible sheaf $\sh{L}_1$. But this contradicts the definition of $V$, which concludes the proof.
\end{proof}

\oldpage[IV-4]{320}

\begin{corollary}[21.13.11]
\label{IV.21.13.11}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $Y$ a closed subset of $X$, $U=X-Y$.
Suppose that the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial and that $U$ is locally factorial; in other terms \sref{IV.21.13.10}, for every $x\in X$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is: $1\textsuperscript{o}$~parafactorial if $x\in Y$; $2\textsuperscript{o}$~factorial if $x\notin Y$.
Then $X$ is locally factorial (in other words, $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is in fact factorial for every $x\in X$).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Suppose on the contrary, and let $y$ be a point of $Y$ such that $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ is not factorial and having the least dimension \emph{minima} amongst the points of $Y$ with this property.
Then, if one sets $T_y=\Spec(\sh{O}_{X,y})$, $U_y=T_y-\{y\}$, the hypothesis that $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ is parafactorial entails $\operatorname{Pic}(U_y)=0$ and $\operatorname{prof}(\sh{O}_{X,y})\geq 2$ \sref{IV.21.13.8}; moreover the choice of $y$ entails that $U_y$ is locally factorial.
But then \sref{IV.21.6.14} proves that $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ is factorial, contrary to the hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.12]
\label{IV.21.13.12}
Let $A$, $B$ be two local Noetherian rings, $\rho:A\to B$ a local homomorphism making $B$ a flat $A$-module.
Then, if $B$ is factorial, so is $A$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
One already knows that $A$ is integral and integrally closed \sref{IV.6.5.4}, hence one can reduce to the case where $\dim(A)\geq 2$, and reason by induction on $n=\dim(A)$.
Let $X=\Spec(A)$, $a$ the closed point of $X$, $X'=\Spec(B)$, $f:X'\to X$ the structural morphism, $U=X-\{a\}$, $U'=f^{-1}(U)$.

The local rings $\sh{O}_{X',x'}$ at the points $x'\in f^{-1}(a)$ are factorial and of dimension $\geq 2$ \sref{IV.6.1.2}, hence parafactorial \sref{IV.21.13.9},~(ii)); one thus concludes \sref{IV.21.13.10} that the couple $(X',f^{-1}(a))$ is parafactorial.
By virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(iii)), this shows that the ring $A$ is parafactorial, hence $\operatorname{prof}(A)\geq 2$ and $\operatorname{Pic}(U)=0$ \sref{IV.21.13.8}.
Finally, the local rings of $U$ being of dimension $<n$, the induction hypothesis on the recursion proves that $U$ is \emph{locally factorial}; the conclusion results then from \sref{IV.21.6.14}.
\end{proof}

\begin{env}[21.13.12.1]
\label{IV.21.13.12.1}
One will note that the statement of \sref{IV.21.13.12} where one replaces ``factorial'' by ``parafactorial'' is no longer exact, as shown by the example where $A=k$ is a field and $B$ the local ring $k[[T_1,T_2]]$.
However, it results from \sref{IV.21.13.12} that, under the conditions of \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(iii)), if $\mathfrak{m}$ denotes the maximal ideal of $A$ and if $\mathfrak{m}B$ is an \emph{ideal of definition} of $B$ (in other words, if $\dim(B/\mathfrak{m}B)=0$), then, when $B$ is parafactorial, so is $A$.
In particular, if the completion $\hat{A}$ of a Noetherian local ring is parafactorial, so is $A$.
\end{env}

\oldpage[IV-4]{321}

\begin{remark}[21.13.13]
\label{IV.21.13.13}
A part of the preceding definitions and results relates to more general considerations on the cohomology of sheaves of commutative groups on a topological space, developed in chap.~III, 3\textsuperscript{e} partie, and we have only given here an independent account for the convenience of the reader.
Indeed, if $X$ is a ringed space, one has a canonical \emph{equivalence} between the category of invertible $\sh{O}_X$-Modules and that of \emph{principal homogeneous sheaves under the sheaf of groups} $\sh{O}_X^*$ \sref{IV.16.5.15}.
This equivalence is defined by making correspond to every invertible $\sh{O}_X$-Module $\sh{L}$ the sheaf $\sh{L}^*=\sh{I}som_{\sh{O}_X}(\sh{O}_X,\sh{L})$ of germs of isomorphisms of $\sh{O}_X$ onto $\sh{L}$; it is immediate that $\sh{L}^*$ is canonically endowed with a structure of principal homogeneous sheaf under $\sh{O}_X^*\cong\sh{I}som_{\sh{O}_X}(\sh{O}_X,\sh{O}_X)$.
The verification that the functor $\sh{L}\mapsto\sh{L}^*$ is an equivalence of categories is immediate.
This being so, let us consider in a general way a topological space $X$; let $Y$ be a closed subset of $X$, set $U=X-Y$, and let $i$ be an integer such that $0\leq i\leq 2$; we will say that the couple $(X,Y)$ is \emph{$i$-pure} for $\sh{G}$ if, for every open $V$ of $X$, the restriction functor $\sh{P}\mapsto\sh{P}|(U\cap V)$, from the category of principal homogeneous sheaves under the sheaf of groups $\sh{G}|V$, to the analogous category under the sheaf of groups $\sh{G}|(U\cap V)$, is faithful ($i=0$), resp.\ fully faithful ($i=1$), resp.\ an equivalence of categories ($i=2$).
In the case where $X$ is a ringed space and $\sh{G}=\sh{O}_X^*$, to say that $(X,Y)$ is $i$-pure thus means for $i=0$, that the homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is injective; for $i=1$, that this homomorphism is bijective; and finally, for $i=2$, that the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial \sref{IV.21.13.3}.

Returning to the general case, recall that the morphisms of the category of principal sheaves under $\sh{G}$ are by definition isomorphisms.
To say that the couple $(X,Y)$ is $0$-pure thus means that for every open $V$ of $X$, the canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{H}^0(V,\sh{G})\to\mathrm{H}^0(U\cap V,\sh{G})$ is injective; to say that the couple $(X,Y)$ is $1$-pure means that the canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{H}^0(V,\sh{G})\to\mathrm{H}^0(U\cap V,\sh{G})$ is bijective (and then the canonical homomorphism $\mathrm{H}^1(V,\sh{G})\to\mathrm{H}^1(U\cap V,\sh{G})$ is injective); finally, one can show that for the couple $(X,Y)$ to be $2$-pure, it is necessary and sufficient that the homomorphisms $\mathrm{H}^i(V,\sh{G})\to\mathrm{H}^i(U\cap V,\sh{G})$ be bijective for $i=0$ and $i=1$.
When $\sh{G}$ is a sheaf of commutative groups, by introducing the cohomology sheaves $\sh{H}_Y^i(\sh{G})$ defined in chap.~III, 3\textsuperscript{e} partie, to say that $(X,Y)$ is $i$-pure for $i\leq 2$ means that $\sh{H}_Y^j(\sh{G})=0$ for $j\leq i$; under this form, the notion generalises immediately for every integer $i$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[21.13.14]
\label{IV.21.13.14}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $(U_\lambda)_{\lambda\in L}$ a decreasing filtering family of open subsets of $X$.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[a)] Every $1$-codimensional cycle $Z$ on $X$ such that there exists an index $\lambda$ for which $Z|U_\lambda$ is locally principal \sref{IV.21.6.7}, is locally principal.
  \item[b)] For every $x\in X$ such that $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 2$ and such that $x$ does not belong to $\bigcap_\lambda U_\lambda$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is parafactorial.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\oldpage[IV-4]{322}

Property $a)$ can also be expressed as follows: if the closed set $N$ of points $x\in X$ where $Z$ is not locally principal is contained in one of the $X-U_\lambda$, then $Z$ is locally principal.
As $X$ is normal, the condition $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\leq 1$ entails that $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is a field or a discrete valuation ring, hence $Z_x$ is principal; hence necessarily $\operatorname{codim}(N,X)\geq 2$ \sref{IV.5.1.3}.
Property $a)$ is therefore equivalent to the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[$a')$] If there exists a closed set $Y$ contained in one of the $X-U_\lambda$, such that $\operatorname{codim}(Y,X)\geq 2$ and such that $Z|X-Y$ is locally principal, then $Z$ is locally principal.
\end{enumerate}

Let us note on the other hand that $b)$ implies $b')$ by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.10}; conversely, if $b')$ is satisfied and if $x\notin U_\lambda$, then $Y=\{x\}$ is contained in $X-U_\lambda$ and one has $\operatorname{codim}(Y,X)\geq 2$, which shows that $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is parafactorial, hence the equivalence of $a')$ and of $b')$.
Let us note that since $X$ is normal, on has, for every closed subset $Y$ of $X$ such that $\operatorname{codim}(Y,X)\geq 2$, $\operatorname{prof}_Y(\sh{O}_X)\geq 2$ \sref{IV.5.8.6}; if one sets $U=X-Y$, the homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is therefore bijective \sref{IV.21.13.4}; as conditions $a')$ and $b')$ are local, on has, for every closed subset $Y$ of $X$ such that $\operatorname{codim}(Y,X)\geq 2$ and $Y\subset X-U_\lambda$, the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial, that is to say that the equivalence of conditions $a')$ and $b')$.

Let us first prove that $a'')$ entails $c)$.
Inversely, let us prove that $b'')$ entails $a'')$.
Let $Z$ be a $1$-codimensional cycle on $X$ such that $Z|U$ is locally principal; the image of $Z|U$ in $\mathfrak{Cl}(U)$ \sref{IV.21.6.11} is the class of a $1$-codimensional cycle $Z_0$ on $U$, which is locally principal.
Since $Z_0$ is locally principal, it is also the case of $Z$ on $U$, by virtue of $a'')$; it results from \sref{IV.21.6.11} that the image of $Z$ in $\mathfrak{Cl}(X)$ is the image of a unique element $\zeta\in\operatorname{Pic}(X)$, and it is clear then that $\zeta_0$ is the image of $\zeta$.

Let us prove first that $a'')$ entails $c)$.
Let $Z$ be a $1$-codimensional cycle on $X$ which is locally principal; by hypothesis $Z|U_\lambda$ is also locally principal.
Conversely $c)$ entails $a'')$: if $Z|U$ is locally principal, the image of $Z|U$ in $\mathfrak{Cl}(U)$ is therefore the class of a $1$-codimensional cycle $Z'$ on $X$ such that $Z|U$ and $Z'|U$ are linearly equivalent.
But since $U$ is schematically dense in $X$ by the isomorphism $\mathfrak{Z}^1(X)\to\mathfrak{Z}^1(U)$, $Z$ and $Z'$ are linearly equivalent, and since $Z'$ is locally principal by virtue of $a'')$, hence so is $Z$.

\begin{corollary}[21.13.15]
\label{IV.21.13.15}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme and normal, $S$ a subset of $X$.
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[a)] Every $1$-codimensional cycle on $X$ which is principal at the points of $S$ is locally principal.
  \item[b)] For every $x\in X$ which is not a generisation of a point of $S$ (in other words, such that $\overline{\{x\}}\cap S=\emp$) and which is such that $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 2$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is parafactorial.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

It is clear that the set $S'$ of the points of $X$ which are generisations of points of $S$ is the intersection of the open neighbourhoods of $S$.
One can reduce to the case where $X$ is Noetherian (the properties $a)$ and $b)$ being local on $X$); as every $1$-codimensional cycle on $X$ which is principal at the points of $S$ is also such in a neighbourhood open of $S$, one sees therefore that, in the statement of \sref{IV.21.13.15}, if $S=\{z\}$, the conditions $a)$ and $b)$ are also equivalent to the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[c)] Every $1$-codimensional cycle on $X$ whose support does not contain $z$ is locally principal.
\end{enumerate}
If moreover $\operatorname{Pic}(X)=0$, one concludes that this condition entails that every $1$-codimensional cycle whose support does not contain $z$ is \emph{principal}.

\oldpage[IV-4]{323}

\begin{remark}[21.13.16]
\label{IV.21.13.16}
Under the general conditions of \sref{IV.21.13.14}, suppose moreover that one has $\varinjlim\operatorname{Pic}(U_\lambda)=0$.
Then condition $a)$ of \sref{IV.21.13.14} is also equivalent to the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[c)] Every $1$-codimensional cycle on $X$ whose support is contained in one of the sets $X-U_\lambda$ is locally principal.
\end{enumerate}
One can reduce to the case where $X$ is irreducible and all the $U_\lambda$ are non-empty.
It is clear that $a)$ entails $c)$, for if the $1$-codimensional cycle $Z$ on $X$ has its support in $X-U_\lambda$, $Z|U_\lambda=0$, hence $Z|U_\lambda$ is locally principal.
Conversely $c)$ entails $a)$: let $Z$ be a $1$-codimensional cycle on $X$ such that $Z|U_\lambda$ is locally principal; by hypothesis there exists $U_\mu\subset U_\lambda$ such that $D_\mu=\operatorname{div}(f_\mu)$, where $f_\mu$ is a rational function regular on $U_\mu$, and $f_\mu$ can be viewed as a rational function regular on $X$.
If $Z'=\operatorname{cyc}(\operatorname{div}(f_\mu))$, then $Z''=Z-Z'$ has its support in $X-U_\mu$; by virtue of the hypothesis, $Z''$ is locally principal, hence so is the conclusion.

The condition $\varinjlim\operatorname{Pic}(U_\lambda)=0$ is fulfilled in particular if $(U_\lambda)$ is the family of open neighbourhoods of a point $z\in X$, for then for every $\sh{O}_{U_\lambda}$-Module invertible $\sh{L}_\lambda$, there exists $U_\mu\subset U_\lambda$ such that $\sh{L}_\lambda|U_\mu$ is isomorphic to $\sh{O}_{U_\mu}$.
One sees therefore that, in the statement of \sref{IV.21.13.15}, if $S=\{z\}$, the conditions $a)$ and $b)$ are also equivalent to the following:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[c)] Every $1$-codimensional cycle on $X$ whose support does not contain $z$ is locally principal.
\end{enumerate}
If moreover $\operatorname{Pic}(X)=0$, one concludes that this condition entails that every $1$-codimensional cycle whose support does not contain $z$ is \emph{principal}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Ramanujam--Samuel theorem.}
\label{subsection:IV.21.14}

\begin{theorem}[21.14.1]
\label{IV.21.14.1}
\textnormal{(Ramanujam--Samuel)}
Let $A$ be a Noetherian local ring of maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}$, such that its completion $\hat{A}$ is integral and integrally closed.
Let $B$ be a Noetherian ring such that $\dim(B)>\dim(A)$, $\rho:A\to B$ a local homomorphism making $B$ a $A$-algebra \emph{formally smooth} (for the preadic topologies $(\mathbf{0}, 19.3.1)$) and such that the residue field of $B$ is finite over that of $A$.
Then every $1$-codimensional cycle on $\Spec(B)$ which is principal at the point $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}B$, is a $1$-codimensional principal cycle.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $k=A/\mathfrak{m}$ is the residue field of $A$, $B/\mathfrak{m}B$ is a $k$-algebra formally smooth (for its preadic topology) $(\mathbf{0}, 19.3.5)$, hence regular, and in particular integral, in other words $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}B$ is indeed a prime ideal of $B$, which justifies the statement.
Everything evidently reduces to proving that every prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}$ of $B$ not contained in $\mathfrak{p}$ is \emph{principal}.

\oldpage[IV-4]{324}
Let $\hat{A}$, $\hat{B}$ be the completions of $A$ and $B$ respectively, so that the maximal ideal of $\hat{A}$ is $\mathfrak{m}\hat{A}$; one knows $(\mathbf{0}, 19.3.6)$ that $\hat{B}$ is an $\hat{A}$-algebra formally smooth for the adic topologies.
Let $k'$ be the residue field of $\hat{B}$, finite extension of $k$; there exists a homomorphism of local rings $\hat{A}\to C$, where $C$ is a local Noetherian ring which is a \emph{finite} and \emph{flat} $\hat{A}$-module and is such that $C/\mathfrak{m}C$ is isomorphic to $k'$ $(\mathbf{0}_{III}, 10.3.1)$; one deduces then from \sref{IV.7.5.1}, \sref{IV.7.5.3} and \sref{IV.6.5.4},~(ii)) that $C$ is \emph{integral and integrally closed}.
Moreover, $D=\hat{B}\otimes_{\hat{A}}C$ is a complete semi-local ring, composed direct of complete local rings of which one, $D_0$, has for residue field $k'$ (since $k'\otimes_k k'$ is composed direct of local rings of which one is isomorphic to $k'$).
As $D$ is formally smooth over $C$, so is $D_0/\mathfrak{m}D_0$, which is a $k'$-algebra formally smooth, hence is $k'$-isomorphic to an algebra of formal power series $k'[[T_1,\ldots,T_n]]$ $(\mathbf{0}, 19.6.4)$; one concludes, by $(\mathbf{0}, 19.7.1.5)$, that $D_0$ is $C$-isomorphic to $C[[T_1,\ldots,T_n]]$, and hence \emph{integral and integrally closed} (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~V, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~4, prop.~14).
As the morphisms $\Spec(D_0)\to\Spec(\hat{B})$ and $\Spec(D_0)\to\Spec(\hat{B})$ are faithfully flat, one deduces that $B$ and $\hat{B}$ are also \emph{integral and integrally closed} \sref{IV.2.1.13}.
This proves that $\mathfrak{q}D_0$ is divisorial (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~10, prop.~15) and not contained in $\mathfrak{m}D_0$, hence in particular that no prime ideal $\mathfrak{r}_h$ of height $1$ in $D_0$ which contains $\mathfrak{q}D_0$ can be contained in $\mathfrak{m}D_0$; since $\mathfrak{q}D_0$ is principal by faithful flatness $(\mathbf{0}_I, 6.5.1)$.
One concludes that the divisors (in the sense of Bourbaki) of $\mathfrak{q}D_0$ and of a product of powers of the $\mathfrak{r}_h$ are equal (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~4, prop.~5).
As $\mathfrak{q}D_0\cap B=\mathfrak{q}$, one deduces by faithful flatness that $\mathfrak{q}$ is principal \sref{IV.2.5.2}, using the fact that $B$ is a local ring.

One is thus reduced to proving the theorem in the particular case where $A$ is \emph{complete} and $B=A[[T_1,\ldots,T_n]]$.
Let us show first that one can reduce to the case where $n=1$.
Indeed, we proceed by induction on $n$, and let $f\in\mathfrak{q}$ be an element not belonging to $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}B$; it will suffice to show that there exists an $A$-automorphism $\sigma$ of $B$ such that $\sigma(f)$ does not belong to the ideal $\mathfrak{p}'=\mathfrak{p}+BT_1+\ldots+BT_{n-1}$: indeed, if $B'=A[[T_1,\ldots,T_{n-1}]]$, $B'$ is complete and has for maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}B'+B'T_1+\ldots+B'T_{n-1}=\mathfrak{m}'$.
One has $B=B'[[T_n]]$ and $\mathfrak{p}'=\mathfrak{m}'B$, and it will result from the induction hypothesis (taking into account that $B'$ is integral and integrally closed) that the ideal $\sigma(\mathfrak{q})$ is principal in $B$, hence also $\mathfrak{q}$.
Now, if $\bar{f}\in k[[T_1,\ldots,T_n]]$ is the image of $f$ (``reduced series'' of $f$), one knows (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \textsection~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~7, lemme~3) that there is an $A$-automorphism $\sigma$ of $B$ such that $(\sigma(f))(0,\ldots,0,T_n)\neq 0$, which implies evidently $\sigma(f)\notin\mathfrak{p}'$.

\oldpage[IV-4]{325}
Suppose then $n=1$ and write $T$ in place of $T_1$, so that $B=A[[T]]$.
It suffices to show that in the polynomial ring $A[T]$, the ideal $\mathfrak{q}\cap A[T]$ is principal; indeed, let $f_0$ be an element of $\mathfrak{q}$ not belonging to $\mathfrak{m}B$; this is a power series whose reduced series $\bar{f}_0$ is non-zero; hence, by virtue of the preparation theorem (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \textsection~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~8, prop.~5), for every $f\in\mathfrak{q}$, there exist $g\in B$ and a polynomial $r\in A[T]$ such that $f=gf_0+r$ and one has thus $r\in\mathfrak{q}\cap A[T]$; on the other hand (\emph{loc.\ cit.}, prop.~6) there exist a distinguished non-constant polynomial $F_0\in A[T]$ and an invertible $u\in B$ such that $f_0=uF_0$, hence one also has $F_0\in\mathfrak{q}\cap A[T]$, which proves that $\mathfrak{q}$ is generated by $\mathfrak{q}\cap A[T]=\mathfrak{q}_1$.
Since $B$ is flat over $A[T]$ $(\mathbf{0}_I, 7.3.3)$, it results from Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~VII, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~10, prop.~15, that $\mathfrak{q}_1$ is a prime ideal of height $1$ in $A[T]$.
Moreover, one has necessarily $\mathfrak{q}_1\cap A=0$; otherwise, $\mathfrak{q}_1\cap A$ would necessarily be of height $\geq 1$, and it would result from \sref{IV.5.5.3} that one would have $\mathfrak{q}_1=(\mathfrak{q}_1\cap A)A[T]$.
But then, since $\mathfrak{q}_1\cap A\subset\mathfrak{m}$, one would have $\mathfrak{q}_1\subset\mathfrak{m}A[T]$, contrary to the hypothesis on $\mathfrak{q}$.
If $K$ is the field of fractions of $A$, $\mathfrak{q}_1K[T]$ is therefore a prime ideal distinct from $0$ and of $K[T]$, hence of the form $h\cdot K[T]$, where $h(T)=T^m+a_1T^{m-1}+\ldots+a_m$ with $m\geq 1$ and the $a_i\in K$, $h$ being irreducible in $K[T]$.
But one has seen above that there exists in $\mathfrak{q}_1$ a distinguished non-constant polynomial $F_0\in A[T]$.
If $t$ is the class of $T$ in $K[T]/\mathfrak{q}_1K[T]$, $t$ is therefore a root of the polynomial $F_0$ in an extension of $K$ and hence $h$ divides $F_0$ \emph{in} $K[T]$; but since $h$ and $F_0$ are unitary, this entails that the coefficients $a_i$ of $h$ are integers over $A$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~V, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~3, prop.~11), hence belong to $A$ since $A$ is integrally closed.
In other words, one has $h\in A[\Gamma]\cap\mathfrak{q}_1K[T]=\mathfrak{q}_1$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~II, \textsection~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~5, prop.~11); as every polynomial $g\in\mathfrak{q}_1$ is divisible by $h$ in $K[T]$ and $h$ is unitary, the coefficients of $g/h$ belong to $A$, hence $\mathfrak{q}_1=h\cdot A[T]$.
Q.E.D.
\end{proof}

The statement \sref{IV.21.14.1} is \emph{equivalent} to the following:

\begin{corollary}[21.14.2]
\label{IV.21.14.2}
Under the hypotheses of \sref{IV.21.14.1} on $A$, $B$ and $\rho$, for every prime ideal $\mathfrak{q}$ of $B$ not contained in $\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}B$ and such that $\dim(B_\mathfrak{q})\geq 2$, the ring $B_\mathfrak{q}$ is parafactorial.
In particular, if $\dim(B\otimes_A k)\geq 0$ (i.e.\ $\dim B>\dim A$ $((\mathbf{0}, 19.7.1)$ and $\sref{IV.6.1.1}))$, the ring $B$ is parafactorial.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, one has seen in the proof of \sref{IV.21.14.1} that the conditions of \sref{IV.21.14.1} entail that $B$ is integral and integrally closed; the equivalence of the statements \sref{IV.21.14.1} and \sref{IV.21.14.2} results then from \sref{IV.21.13.15} applied to $X=\Spec(B)$ and $S=\{\mathfrak{p}\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[21.14.3]
\label{IV.21.14.3}
Let $S$ be a normal prescheme, $f:X\to S$ a smooth morphism.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[(i)] If $S$ is locally Noetherian (hence also $X$), then, for every $x\in X$ such that $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 2$ and such that $x$ is not the maximal point of its fibre $f^{-1}(f(x))$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is parafactorial.
  Every $1$-codimensional cycle $Z$ on $X$ such that $\Supp(Z)$ does not contain any irreducible component of a fibre $f^{-1}(s)=X_s$ is locally principal.
  \item[(ii)] Let $Y$ be a closed subset of $X$ not containing any irreducible component of a fibre $X_s$, and such that for every maximal point $\eta$ of $S$, $Y_\eta=Y\cap X_\eta$ is of codimension $\geq 2$ in $X_\eta$.
  Then the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

One will note that the conditions of~(ii) are satisfied if $Y$ is a closed subset of $X$ such that, for every $s\in S$, $Y_s=Y\cap X_s$ is of codimension $\geq 2$ in $X_s$.

\oldpage[IV-4]{326}

\begin{proof}
To prove \sref{IV.21.14.3}, let us note first that under the hypotheses of~(i), if one sets $Y=\overline{\{x\}}$, there exists a neighbourhood $V$ of $x$ in $X$ such that $Y\cap V$ and $V$ verify the conditions of~(ii): indeed, it results from the hypothesis and from \sref{IV.9.5.3} that one can take $V$ such that $V\cap Y$ does not contain any irreducible component of an $X_s$; on the other hand, for every maximal point $\eta$ of $S$ such that $Y\cap X_\eta\neq\emp$, the points of $Y\cap X_\eta$ are specialisations of $x$, hence there is at most a single point $z$ and one has $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,z})\geq 2$, and as $\sh{O}_{X,z}=\sh{O}_{X_\eta,z}$ (the reduction being at point $\eta$ by virtue of \sref{IV.2.1.13}), $Y\cap X_\eta$ is indeed of codimension $\geq 2$.
As the couple $(V,Y\cap V)$ is then parafactorial by virtue of~(ii), one concludes from \sref{IV.21.13.10} that $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is parafactorial, i.e.\ the first assertion of~(i).
To prove the second, one can reduce to the case where $Z=\{\overline{z}\}$, where $z\in X^{(1)}$; as $\sh{O}_{X,z}$ is a discrete valuation ring, $Z$ is therefore principal at the point $z$; for every other point $x$ of $\Supp(Z)$, one has $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 2$ and $x$ is not the maximal point of its fibre by hypothesis, hence $\sh{O}_{X,x}$ is parafactorial.
We then apply \sref{IV.21.13.15} taking for $S$ the set formed by $z$ and the maximal points of the fibres of $f$; it is clear that $Z$ is principal at the points of $S$; as on the other hand condition $b)$ of \sref{IV.21.13.15} is evidently satisfied, one concludes that $Z$ is locally principal.

We are therefore reduced to proving~(ii).
Set $U=X-Y$ and let $j:U\to X$ be the canonical injection.
As the hypotheses and the conclusion are local on $S$ and on $X$ by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(i)), one can reduce to the case where $S$ and $X$ are affine, $f$ being then of finite presentation.
As the fibres of $f$ are regular, the hypothesis on $Y$ entails that, for every point $x\in Y$, $\operatorname{prof}(\sh{O}_{X_{f(x)},x})=\dim(\sh{O}_{X_{f(x)},x})\geq 1$; it results from $(19\,9\,8)$ that the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is \emph{injective}.
Moreover, replacing $Y$ by a larger closed subset following the method of the proof of $(19\,9\,8)$, and using \sref{IV.21.13.6},~(ii)), one can suppose that $Y$ is defined by an ideal of \emph{finite type} of the ring of $X$, hence the open $U=X-Y$ is \emph{quasi-compact} and \emph{quasi-separated} and the closed set $Y$ is \emph{constructible}.

\oldpage[IV-4]{327}
To prove~(ii), it suffices, for every invertible $\sh{O}_U$-Module $\sh{L}$ and every point $x\in Y$, to establish the existence of a neighbourhood open $V$ of $x$ in $X$ such that: $1\textsuperscript{o}$~the canonical homomorphism $\sh{O}_V\to(j_V)_*(\sh{O}_{U\cap V})$ is surjective; $2\textsuperscript{o}$~there exists an invertible $\sh{O}_V$-Module $\sh{L}'_V$ such that $\sh{L}'_V|(U\cap V)\cong\sh{L}|(U\cap V)$ $(\sref{IV.21.13.5}$ and $\sref{IV.21.13.3})$.
Set $s=f(x)$; we will show that one can reduce to the case where $S=S_0=\Spec(\sh{O}_{S,s})$.
Let $(S_\nu)$ be a fundamental system of affine open neighbourhoods of $s$ in $S$, and set $X_\nu=f^{-1}(S_\nu)$, $Y_\nu=Y\cap X_\nu$, $U_\nu=X_\nu-Y_\nu$, $j_\nu:U_\nu\to X_\nu$ being the canonical injection; $X_0=X\times_SS_0$ is then a projective limit of the $X_\nu$ \sref{IV.8.1.2},~$a)$); suppose the proposition true for the morphism $f_0:X_0\to S_0$ and set $Y_0=Y\cap X_0$, and set $U_0=X_0-Y_0$, $j_0:U_0\to X_0$ being the canonical injection.
The canonical projection $p_\nu:X_0\to X_\nu$ being an affine morphism, one has $(j_0)_*(\sh{O}_{U_0})=p_\nu^*((j_\nu)_*(\sh{O}_{U_\nu}))$ $(\sref[II]{II.1.5.2})$ and the canonical homomorphism $\rho_0:\sh{O}_{X_0}\to(j_0)_*(\sh{O}_{U_0})$ is nothing other than $p_\nu^*(\rho_\nu)$, where $\rho_\nu:\sh{O}_{X_\nu}\to(j_\nu)_*(\sh{O}_{U_\nu})$ is the canonical homomorphism; as by hypothesis $\rho_0$ is surjective, so is $\rho_\nu$ for $\nu$ large enough \sref{IV.8.5.2},~(ii)); moreover $(8.5.5)$ one can suppose that $\sh{L}'_\nu$ is invertible.
Finally, as the $U_\nu$ are quasi-compact and quasi-separated, and as $\sh{L}'_0|U_0=\sh{L}_0$, there exists $\nu$ large enough such that $\sh{L}'_\nu|U_\nu$ and $\sh{L}|U_\nu$ are isomorphic \sref{IV.8.5.2.5}.

One is thus reduced to the case where $S=\Spec(A)$, $X=\Spec(B)$, where $A$ is a local ring.
Since $X$ is normal and $f$ smooth, $S$ is normal \sref{IV.17.5.7}, hence $A$ is \emph{integral and integrally closed}.
Consider then $A$ as an inductive limit of its sub-$\bb{Z}$-algebras of \emph{finite type}; as the integral closure of such a sub-$\bb{Z}$-algebra is a sub-ring of $A$ and is also a $\bb{Z}$-algebra of finite type $(7.8.3,\text{ (ii), (iii) and (vi)})$, $A$ is an inductive filtering limit of sub-$\bb{Z}$-algebras of type $A_\lambda$ of $A$ which are rings \emph{integrally closed}.
By virtue of $(1.8.4.2)$, there exists an index $\lambda$ and an $A_\lambda$-algebra of finite type $B_\lambda$ such that $B=B_\lambda\otimes_{A_\lambda}A$ up to an $A$-isomorphism.
Set $S_\lambda=\Spec(A_\lambda)$, $X_\lambda=\Spec(B_\lambda)$; if $p_\lambda:X\to X_\lambda$ is the canonical projection, one can moreover suppose (since $Y$ is \emph{constructible}) that $Y=p_\lambda^{-1}(Y_\lambda)$, where $Y_\lambda$ is a closed subset of $X_\lambda$ $(8.3.11)$.
Let $f_\lambda$ be the morphism $X_\lambda\to S_\lambda$; since $f$ is smooth, one can also suppose that $f_\lambda$ is smooth \sref{IV.17.7.8}; finally, the image of the generic point $\eta$ of $S$ in $S_\lambda$ is the generic point $\eta_\lambda$ of $S_\lambda$, and one has $\operatorname{codim}((Y_\lambda)_{\eta_\lambda},(X_\lambda)_{\eta_\lambda})=\operatorname{codim}(Y_\eta,X_\eta)\geq 2$ by virtue of \sref{IV.6.1.4}.
One sees therefore that $S_\lambda$, $X_\lambda$ and $Y_\lambda$ verify all the hypotheses of~(ii), and on setting $X_\mu=X_\lambda\times_{S_\lambda}S_\mu$ for $\lambda\leq\mu$, it is the same of $S_\mu$, $X_\mu$ and $Y_\mu$.
Let us show that if the couple $(X_\mu,Y_\mu)$ is parafactorial for every $\mu\geq\lambda$, then it is the same of $(X,Y)$.
Indeed, let $U=X-Y$, $U_\lambda=X_\lambda-Y_\lambda$, $j:U\to X$, $j_\lambda:U_\lambda\to X_\lambda$ the canonical injections; the projection $p_\lambda:X\to X_\lambda$ being an affine morphism, one has $j_*(\sh{O}_U)=p_\lambda^*((j_\lambda)_*(\sh{O}_{U_\lambda}))$ $(\sref[II]{II.1.5.2})$, and hence the canonical homomorphism $\rho:\sh{O}_X\to j_*(\sh{O}_U)$ is nothing other than $p_\lambda^*(\rho_\lambda)$; this latter being by hypothesis bijective, so is $\rho$.
On the other hand, for every invertible $\sh{O}_U$-Module $\sh{L}$, there exists a $\mu\geq\lambda$ and an invertible $\sh{O}_{U_\mu}$-Module $\sh{L}_\mu$ such that $\sh{L}=p_\mu^*(\sh{L}_\mu)$ (denoting by $p'_\mu:U\to U_\mu$ the restriction of $p_\mu$) $(8.5.2$ and $8.5.5)$, $U_\lambda$ being quasi-compact since $X_\lambda$ is Noetherian.
By hypothesis, there exists an $\sh{O}_{X_\mu}$-Module invertible $\sh{L}'_\mu$ such that $\sh{L}'_\mu|U_\mu$ is isomorphic to $\sh{L}_\mu$; $\sh{L}'=p_\mu^*(\sh{L}'_\mu)$ is then an $\sh{O}_X$-Module invertible such that $\sh{L}'|U$ is isomorphic to $\sh{L}$, which proves our assertion.

One is thus reduced to proving~(ii) when $A$ is a $\bb{Z}$-algebra of finite type integrally closed; as the local rings $\sh{O}_{S,s}$ of $S$ are then rings which are excellent integrally closed (7.8.3,~(ii), (iii) and (vii)), their \emph{completions} are also integrally closed.

\oldpage[IV-4]{328}
For every $x\in Y$, the ring $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ is an $\sh{O}_{S,s}$-algebra formally smooth for the preadic topologies \sref{IV.17.5.3}; since $Y_s$ does not contain any irreducible component of $X_s$, one has $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})>\dim(\sh{O}_{S,s})$ \sref{IV.17.5.8}.
If $s\neq\eta$, one has $\dim(\sh{O}_{S,s})\geq 1$; if on the contrary $s=\eta$, one has by hypothesis $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 2$; hence in all cases $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq 2$.
Moreover, the prime ideal of $\sh{O}_{X,y}$ corresponding to the point $x$ is not contained in $\mathfrak{m}_s\sh{O}_{X,y}$ since $Y$ does not contain any irreducible component of $X_s$.
Finally, since $y$ is a closed point of $X_s$, $k(y)$ is a finite extension of $k(s)$ $(\sref[I]{I.6.4.2})$.
In all cases, one can apply to $A=\sh{O}_{X,y}$ and $B_\mathfrak{q}=\sh{O}_{X,x}$ the result of \sref{IV.21.14.2}, which finishes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[21.14.4]
\label{IV.21.14.4}
(i) It is possible that the statements \sref{IV.21.14.1} and \sref{IV.21.14.2} remain valid \emph{without hypothesis} on the residue field of $B$.
Stated with this generality, the result would be equivalent, by virtue of \sref{IV.21.13.15} and \sref{IV.21.13.12.1}, to the following: let $A$ be a Noetherian local ring, complete, integral and integrally closed, $B$ a Noetherian local ring which is an $A$-algebra formally smooth, such that $\dim(B)>\dim(A)$; then $B$ is parafactorial.

(ii) We will see in chap.~VI, using a technique of ``finite descent'' applied to the morphism $Y'\to Y=\Spec(A)$, where $Y'$ is the normalised of $Y$, that the conclusion of \sref{IV.21.14.1} (or of \sref{IV.21.14.2}) remains valid on replacing the hypothesis that $\hat{A}$ is integrally closed by the hypothesis that $\hat{A}$ is \emph{reduced}, provided that $\dim B\geq\dim A+2$.
If one replaces this last condition by $\dim B\geq\dim A+3$, one can even suppress the hypothesis that $\hat{A}$ is reduced.
Similarly, the conclusion of \sref{IV.21.14.3} remains valid on replacing the hypothesis that $X$ is normal by the hypothesis that $X$ is reduced, provided that $\dim(\sh{O}_{X,x})\geq\dim(\sh{O}_{S,f(x)})+2$.

(iii) In chap.~III, 3\textsuperscript{e} partie, one proves that if $f:X\to S$ is a smooth morphism, $Y$ a locally constructible closed subset of $X$ such that, for every $s\in S$, one has (with the conditions of \sref{IV.21.14.3})) $\operatorname{codim}(Y_s,X_s)\geq 3$, then the couple $(X,Y)$ is parafactorial (\cite[XII, 4.8]{41}).
This conclusion is no longer valid when one supposes only that $\operatorname{codim}(Y_s,X_s)\geq 2$ for every $s\in S$ and when one does not suppose $X$ reduced.
Take for example $k$ a field, $A=k[T]/(T^2)$, algebra of dual numbers over $k$, $S=\Spec(A)$, $X=\Spec(A[T_1,T_2])$ $(T_1,T_2$ indeterminate), so that $X=\bb{V}_S^2$, $Y$ being the ``zero section'' of this fibre; if $s$ is the unique closed point of $S$, one has $X_s=\bb{V}_k^2$ and $Y_s$ is the ``zero section'' of $X_s$.
To see that the couple $(X,Y)$ is not parafactorial, it suffices to show that the ring $B=A[T_1,T_2]_\mathfrak{m}$, where $\mathfrak{m}$ is the ideal $(T_1)+(T_2)$ which defines $Y$, is not parafactorial \sref{IV.21.13.10}.
Let $B_0=B_{\mathrm{red}}$, and denote by $U$ and $U_0$ the complements of the closed point in $\Spec(B)$ and $\Spec(B_0)$; by reasoning as in \sref{IV.21.13.9}, one has the exact sequence, extension of $(21.13.9.1)$
\[
  \Gamma(U,\sh{O}_U)^*\to\Gamma(U_0,\sh{O}_{U_0})^*\to\mathrm{H}^1(U_0,\sh{O}_{U_0})\to\operatorname{Pic}(U)\to\operatorname{Pic}(U_0).
\]
Now, one has $\Gamma(U,\sh{O}_U)=B$, $\Gamma(U_0,\sh{O}_{U_0})=B_0$ since $\operatorname{prof}(B)=\operatorname{prof}(B_0)=2$ \sref{IV.5.10.5}.
Furthermore $\operatorname{Pic}(U_0)=0$ since $B_0$ is factorial, and $\mathrm{H}^1(U_0,\sh{O}_{U_0})\neq 0$ \cite[3, Exemple~III-1]{41}; as the homomorphism $B^*\to B_0^*$ is surjective, one concludes that $\operatorname{Pic}(U)\neq 0$, hence $B$ is not parafactorial.
\end{remark}
